
- **Export:** flush autosave → repositories dump → worker zips (streamed) → browser download. Raw `.sqlite` binaries are never the interchange format (schema portability); the Safe-Mode raw download (§8.1) is a rescue hatch, not interchange.
- **Import:** Zod-validate manifest + snapshot (reject unknown `formatVersion` with a friendly error); remap all UUIDs on collision; write samples to OPFS; insert rows; open the project. Import is transactional — a failure mid-way leaves no partial project.

### 9.7 Storage Safeguards

- `navigator.storage.persist()` requested at first run; if refused, show a persistent dismissible warning that the browser may evict data.
- **Quota hard-stop:** before any sample write/bounce, check `navigator.storage.estimate()`; if the write would push usage beyond 90 % of quota, refuse gracefully with a storage-management prompt (Browser mode purge tools) — never corrupt a half-written file (write to temp name, atomic rename on completion).
- **Multi-tab guard:** `navigator.locks.request('bangerbox-db', { ifAvailable: true }, ...)` at startup; on failure show the already-open screen (§8.1). The OPFS SQLite lock makes this mandatory, not cosmetic.

### 9.8 Factory Content & Demo Projects

BangerBox ships free, licence-safe starter content so a new user can make sound
before sourcing any audio of their own.

**Delivery format.** Factory content ships as `.mpcweb` archives (§9.6) — no new
format, no new pipeline, no new dependency. Packs are fetched on demand from
`/factory/`, then installed through the same unpack → Zod-validate → UUID-remap →
OPFS-write → row-insert path as a user import. `public/factory/index.json` is a
Zod-validated catalogue: `{ id, title, kind: 'kit' | 'demo', file, bytes,
description }` per pack. Packs are never base64-inlined in JS (the §12 Phase 3
`demoSample.ts` pluck is inlined; that does not scale and is not the precedent here).

**Provenance.** All shipped audio is generated procedurally by
`scripts/build-factory.mjs` from pure synthesis — no sampled, recorded or
third-party material. The repository is MIT-licensed and publicly deployed, so
shipped audio must be unencumbered; generating it removes provenance risk entirely
and keeps rebuilds byte-reproducible. Curated third-party CC0 material is out of
scope.

**Build.** `npm run build:factory` writes `public/factory/`, which is gitignored and
regenerated ahead of `build` — the same artefact discipline as the §5.6 WASM kernels.
Output MUST be byte-deterministic across rebuilds: a seeded PRNG for any noise
source, fixed zip entry mtimes, and a pinned `exportedAt`/`created_at`/`modified_at`
in every generated snapshot. Samples are 48 kHz mono WAV at 16-bit; total shipped
payload stays under 8 MB.

**Install modes.** A `demo` pack installs as a new project and opens it. A `kit`
pack MERGES into the active project: its programs and samples are inserted, its
sequences, tracks and song entries are discarded. Both modes remap every UUID (§9.6)
and both are transactional in the §9.6 sense — a mid-way failure leaves no partial
project and no orphaned OPFS files.

**Storage.** Installing a pack is a storage-growing write and MUST pass the §9.7
headroom hard-stop, checked against the pack's uncompressed sample payload BEFORE
any OPFS write, refusing gracefully with the Browser-mode purge affordance.

**Caching.** The §2.4 precache glob covers neither `.wav` nor `.mpcweb` and is not
widened. Packs and the catalogue are runtime-cached cache-first in a dedicated cache
(`bangerbox-factory-v*`), kept separate from the precache so the §2.4 stale-precache
prune cannot evict them.

**Content (v1).** Three kits (~40 samples: 808-flavoured, 909-flavoured, acoustic-ish)
and three demo projects — a four-bar boom-bap loop, a house track exercising mixer
automation and a Q-Link-mapped filter sweep (§10.3), and a Song-mode arrangement of
several sequences (§7.9).

**Sample de-duplication.** A demo embeds the audio of the kit it plays, so it opens
standalone — but renders it under the KIT's seed, so the bytes are identical to that
kit pack's. Factory samples install into `/global_library/` (§9.1) addressed by the
SHA-256 of their content, with `project_id = NULL` (§9.3); a pack whose bytes are
already stored adopts the existing row instead of writing a second copy. A user who
installs a kit and its demo therefore stores one copy of each sound, not two. The
§9.7 hard stop is sized on the de-duplicated write set, so an install that adds
nothing is never refused for space. Sharing applies to FACTORY content only — a user
`.mpcweb` import (§9.6) stays project-scoped, since their project must remain
self-contained. "Purge unused samples" (§8.5.7) consequently judges a global sample
against every project's programs, never just the open one. The shipped payload is
unaffected: each pack is a separate archive and still carries its own audio.

## 10. Hardware Interfacing

### 10.1 BLE-MIDI Transport & Parsing

- `navigator.bluetooth.requestDevice` filtered on the BLE-MIDI service UUID `03B80E5A-EDE8-4B33-A751-6CE34EC4C700`; characteristic `7772E5DB-3868-4112-A1A9-F2669D106BF3`; subscribe to notifications.
- **Packet parsing (pure, unit-tested, `src/core/midi/parser.ts`):** BLE-MIDI framing — header byte (bit 7 set; bits 0–5 = timestamp high), per-message timestamp byte (bit 7 set; bits 0–6 = timestamp low) forming a 13-bit millisecond clock that wraps every 8 192 ms (unwrap against arrival time); **running status** support; multiple messages per packet. v1 handles Note On (velocity 0 ⇒ Note Off), Note Off, Control Change, Pitch Bend; SysEx is skipped safely (never crashes the parser).
- Parsed messages carry a reconstructed `performance.now()`-domain timestamp.

### 10.2 Input Routing, Latency & Execution Flow

- **Note messages** follow the dual path of §7.6: immediate audition (zero added latency, bypasses React entirely) + worker delivery for note-repeat/recording. A configurable **input latency offset** (default 15 ms, settable 0–50 ms in Q-Link Edit's settings pane) is subtracted from BLE timestamps when recording, compensating BLE transmission delay.
- **CC messages** MUST flow: BLE listener → binding lookup → mapped value → **Zustand store action** → sync layer → node. Directly mutating the audio graph from the MIDI listener is forbidden (auditioned _notes_ are the sole exception, and they touch the voice pool, not graph parameters).
- **Pitch bend** applies to the active keygroup program's sounding voices via per-voice `detune` ramps, scaled by the program's `pitchBendRange` (§6; default ±2 semitones). Drum programs ignore pitch bend. Like note audition, this is a voice-pool path (throttled per §10.4), not a store mutation.
- **A bend sums with the voice's programmed pitch; it never overrides it.** A bend — and equally a live §6 pad detune edit or a §7.8 detune automation move — is an _offset in cents_ added on top of whatever the patch is already doing to pitch: pad tune, static pitch mod, the pitch envelope, a pitch-routed LFO, and a keygroup mono glide still in flight. A bend arriving mid-envelope or mid-glide therefore bends the sound the patch is making rather than replacing it, and the envelope or glide continues underneath undisturbed; the bend still stands once that contour's last event has passed. This is what makes bend a performance gesture layered over the programmed sound rather than a competing writer of it — overriding would make bending a gliding note cancel its portamento, which is not what either control means. The offset accordingly rides its own summing input into the voice's pitch, not the same `AudioParam` automation timeline the contour occupies; a live retune and a scheduled contour must never be written to one param, because there the later of the two simply wins (§14 `2026-07-18 (x)`). A single offset per voice is shared by bend and pad detune, so the more recent of the two supersedes the other.

### 10.3 Dynamic Q-Link Architecture (Context-Aware Encoders)

- **Modes** (`useHardwareStore.qLinkMode`): `screen` (encoders map to the currently focused UI panel — components register their parameters via a `useQLinkFocus(params[])` hook into `useUIStore`'s focus registry; e.g. opening a Delay insert maps knobs to Time/Feedback/Mix/Tone), `pad` (selected pad's Pitch / Filter Cutoff / Amp Attack / Amp Release by default), `program` (program macros), `project` (global macros: master level, global swing, master filter).
- **Binding schema (binding):**

```ts
interface QLinkBinding {
  encoderIndex: number; // 0..15 (4 physical by default)
  cc: number; // raw CC number from ccMappings
  targetStore: 'mixer' | 'program' | 'transport' | 'project';
  targetParameterPath: string; // §7.8 registry address
  minValue: number;
  maxValue: number;
  curve: 'linear' | 'log';
  mode: 'absolute' | 'relative'; // relative = two's-complement increment encoders
}
```

- **Execution flow (strict):** CC in → look up binding for current mode → scale into `[min,max]` per curve → dispatch to the target store action (transient during turn, commit on 250 ms idle) → sync layer updates the node → UI reacts concurrently. Bindings persist per mode in `app_settings`.

### 10.4 ESP32 / DIY Controller Constraints

- **CC jitter throttling:** per-CC coalescing — keep only the latest value, apply at most every `CC_THROTTLE_MS` (rAF-aligned), with ±1 value hysteresis, preventing render thrash and AudioParam spam from noisy analogue pots (software-side even if the firmware has hysteresis).
- **Connection lifecycle:** listen for `gattserverdisconnected`; set `connectionState: 'reconnecting'`; attempt automatic `device.gatt.connect()` retries with backoff (3 attempts) before prompting. A drop MUST NOT crash the graph, pause playback, or lose Q-Link bindings.
- **Windows pairing quirk:** the connect dialog includes a help note telling Windows users to pair the ESP32 in **Windows Settings → Bluetooth** _before_ using Connect in the app.
- **Timestamping:** always schedule from reconstructed BLE timestamps (§10.1) — never "on receipt" — feeding the lookahead engine for recording accuracy.

### 10.5 Roadmap (Recorded, Not In Scope)

Web MIDI (USB) input; MIDI clock sync in/out; MIDI export (.mid); phase-vocoder stretch; algorithmic reverb presets; audio input tracks; tempo (BPM) automation within a sequence.

## 11. Testing & Verification Strategy

### 11.1 Unit Tests (Vitest, `happy-dom`)

Mandatory coverage for all pure logic: PPQN/tempo maths, swing/quantise/groove functions, loop wrapping + lookahead windowing, mod-matrix evaluation, BLE-MIDI parser (framing, running status, timestamp unwrap, malformed input), WAV encoder (golden bytes), fader/pan laws, ring buffer (concurrent read/write invariants), Zod schemas (accept/reject fixtures), undo core, `.mpcweb` snapshot round-trip (in-memory).

### 11.2 DSP Verification (OfflineAudioContext)

Each DSP kernel and effect ships a renders-under-`OfflineAudioContext` test (run in the Playwright browser context, §11.4, since Node lacks Web Audio): render a known input through the node/kernel and assert numeric properties (RMS within tolerance, spectral peak location, latency sample count, silence after release). Reference signals are synthesised in-test — a tone, an impulse, a shaped burst built in code — not loaded from committed buffers; there is no fixtures directory. A stored golden may only be introduced where a synthesised signal genuinely cannot express the assertion, and its provenance must be recorded alongside it.

### 11.3 Worker & Worklet Mocking

- The DB worker is bypassed in unit tests via an injected in-memory driver behind the same `IDatabaseDriver` interface (repositories never touch `postMessage` directly).
- The scheduler worker's logic is a pure class (`SchedulerCore`) instantiated directly in tests with a fake clock; the worker file is a thin message shell.
- Worklet-dependent components mock the node wrappers; `src/test/mocks/` provides a fake `AudioContext` sufficient for factory lifecycle tests (create/destroy call accounting).

### 11.4 Real-Browser Smoke (Playwright, system Edge)

`scripts/browser-smoke.mjs` (npm `test:e2e`) against the dev server: asserts `crossOriginIsolated === true`; boots the app; passes the start gate; creates a project; loads a bundled test sample; triggers pads and asserts non-silent output via an `AnalyserNode` probe; runs the create/destroy churn heap check (§5.3); records a two-bar sequence with the fake clock accelerated; exports and re-imports a `.mpcweb`; **fails on any console error**. Screenshot artefacts are git-ignored.

### 11.5 Performance Budgets (Review-Gated)

- Touch-to-sound < 30 ms (UI path); zero audible underruns during 8 tracks + 4 inserts playback on the target tablet; UI holds 60 fps with all meters live.
- **No bundle-size or load-time budget applies.** Both are deliberately unconstrained (human developer's decision, §14 2026-07-18 (j)): the app is local-first and installed, so payload size and cold-start duration are not user-facing costs worth trading features or clarity against. Do not reintroduce a size limit, a cold-load target, a size or timing gate in `npm run verify`, or code-splitting/deferred-loading undertaken to hit either. Splitting and lazy loading done for genuine load-order or correctness reasons (the wasm/sqlite assets already load on demand) remain fine. The remaining budgets above are _runtime_ qualities — how the instrument feels once it is running — and those stay binding.
- A dev-only perf HUD (frame time, audio underrun counter via `audioContext.outputLatency` monitoring, voice count) toggles with a keyboard shortcut.

## 12. Implementation Phases

_Each phase is a strict boundary: the agent executes it autonomously end-to-end (§13.3), and MUST satisfy every exit criterion — including the Multi-Lens Review (§3.5) and the listed tests — before the next phase begins. UI beyond stubs is deferred to Phase 7 except where a phase names it._

**Phase 0 — Toolchain & Scaffold.**
Objective: a verified empty shell. Deliverables: `git init`; Vite + React + TS strict scaffold; Tailwind 4 + token file; ESLint/Prettier; `vite.config.ts` per §2.3; PWA manifest + `sw.ts` + update prompt; capability gate screen (§2.1); constants registry; Vitest + Playwright harness (smoke asserts `crossOriginIsolated`); `npm run build:wasm` pipeline with a trivial AssemblyScript kernel proving the worklet-module-transfer path (§5.6.2); enforcement scripts `check:deps`, `check:lang`, `check:stubs` aggregated as `npm run verify` (§13.6).
Exit: `dev`, `build`, `preview`, `test`, `test:e2e`, `lint`, `type-check`, `verify` all green; installable offline PWA shell.

**Phase 1 — Storage Foundation.**
Objective: durable data layer. Deliverables: OPFS wrapper; DB worker + typed RPC + write queue; migration engine + v1 DDL (§9.3); repositories with paginated queries; multi-tab guard; `storage.persist()` + quota checks; Safe Mode skeleton.
Exit: repository round-trip unit tests (in-memory driver) + real-OPFS smoke test creating/reading a project.

**Phase 2 — State & Undo.**
Objective: the brain. Deliverables: all eight stores (§4.2) with actions + Zod validation; transient/commit channels; undo core; autosave queue; project load/hydrate; sync-layer skeleton with subscriber registration.
Exit: store unit tests incl. undo coalescing and autosave debounce (fake timers); hydration test from a fixture DB.

**Phase 3 — Audio Core.**
Objective: sound out. Deliverables: context bootstrap + start gate; node factory + destroys; full mixer graph (§5.2) incl. returns/master/monitor bus; insert wrapper + native effects (`eq4`, `filter`, `delay`, `compressor`, `saturator`, `reverb` v1); voice pool + choke + stealing; pad playback from OPFS samples; meter worklet + SAB + canvas meters; metronome; minimal test UI (pad grid stub + mixer stub).
Exit: audible end-to-end path; meters reflect real peaks; churn test leak-free; OfflineAudioContext effect assertions.

**Phase 4 — Sequencer & Recording.**
Objective: the pulse. Deliverables: scheduler worker + clock sync + playhead SAB; events diff protocol; swing; loop; note repeat; recording (count-in, overdub/replace, live erase); quantise; automation engine + parameter registry; song-mode playback maths.
Exit: timing unit suite (incl. loop-boundary and song-transition scheduling); record-then-playback smoke test.

**Phase 5 — Programs & Sound Design.**
Objective: the instrument. Deliverables: full §6 schemas live (layers, AHDSR application, LFOs, mod matrix, keygroups incl. ≥4 concurrent, glide); program CRUD + persistence; Program Edit mode (functional, unpolished); arpeggiator.
Exit: mod-matrix evaluator tests; velocity-layer switching audible test; keygroup pitch-accuracy offline render test.

**Phase 6 — Sample Pipeline & Heavy DSP.**
Objective: the sampler. Deliverables: import/decode/standardise pipeline; Sample Edit mode with waveform canvas + all tools; `transientDetect` kernel + chop; `granularStretch` kernel + warp mode + stretch render; Looper; bounce/mixdown (§9.5); `.mpcweb` export/import; Browser mode with tagging/audition/drag-to-pad; remaining WASM effects (`multibandComp`, `fdnReverb`, `limiter`) with PDC.
Exit: kernel golden-output tests; pack/unpack round-trip smoke; transient chop accuracy fixture test.

**Phase 7 — Full UI Assembly & Polish.**
Objective: the surface. Deliverables: all 12 modes complete and polished per §8 (Grid editor, Mute, Mixer, Pad Perform, XYFX, Q-Link Edit, Song, Main dashboard); motion/tactility standards; accessibility pass across every mode; perf HUD; wake lock.
Exit: full Multi-Lens sweep of every mode; perf budgets (§11.5) measured and met; zero dead controls (§3.4).

**Phase 8 — Hardware & Q-Link Ecosystem.**
Objective: the controller. Deliverables: BLE transport + parser; dual-path note routing + latency offset; CC throttle; Q-Link runtime (all four modes) + Screen-mode focus registry; auto-reconnect; Windows pairing helper; binding persistence.
Exit: parser unit suite; simulated-stream jitter/reconnect tests; live hardware session sign-off with the human developer.

## 13. Agent Execution Protocols

### 13.1 Protocol Alpha — Inter-Session Handover

Each phase may run in a fresh session with no memory of previous ones. At phase completion the agent MUST regenerate `docs/dev/PHASE_HANDOVER.md` containing: the Locked Decisions (§1.3) restated; current DDL snapshot; repository method signatures; store interfaces as implemented; worker/worklet message protocol versions; component tree topography; kernel inventory; deliberate stubs/technical debt. A new session MUST parse this spec **and** the handover before writing any code, and MUST reuse established patterns rather than inventing parallel ones.

**Continuation prompt mandate:** after the handover is written and the phase branch is merged (§13.3.4), the agent MUST end its final message with the **next** phase's session prompt — generated from the canonical template in §13.7 with its placeholders filled — inside a raw fenced markdown block, ready to paste into a fresh session with zero additional context. Then stop. This self-perpetuating chain is the primary continuity mechanism; the handover document is its payload.

### 13.2 Protocol Beta — Autonomous TDD

For every domain feature: write the failing tests first (unit or offline-render per §11), implement, run the suite, and only then build dependent layers. Halt for the human only if tests still fail after the two permitted correction attempts (§13.4) or if fixing them would contradict the handover's locked schema.

### 13.3 Protocol Gamma — Autonomous Execution

**13.3.1** Within a phase the agent proceeds continuously without asking permission between tasks, self-correcting lint/type/test failures up to the two-strike limit.
**13.3.2 Halt & Query thresholds** (the only reasons to stop): genuine architectural ambiguity where guessing risks structural debt; scope bleeding into a future phase or §1.4 non-goal; destructive schema/data changes beyond the migration pattern; a needed dependency outside the closed matrix (§2.2); phase completion.
**13.3.3 Patch hygiene:** surgical edits only; never write truncation placeholders ("… rest unchanged") to disk; keep the build green at every commit; commit messages describe _what/why_, never internal process.

**13.3.4 Concurrent-Agent Worktree Discipline.** Multiple agents may work on this repository concurrently. All phase work therefore happens in a dedicated **git worktree**, never in the main checkout:

- Create branch `phase-<N>-<slug>` and worktree `.claude/worktrees/phase-<N>` (this path is gitignored and excluded from the test sweep, §2.3). Every file operation for the phase uses absolute paths inside that worktree.
- **Phase 0 bootstrap exception:** the repository does not exist yet — first `git init` + an initial commit on `main` in the project root (`.gitignore`, this spec as-is), then immediately create the phase-0 worktree and do all scaffold work there.
- Each worktree runs its **own `npm install`**. No junctions/symlinks to the root `node_modules`; if one is ever created for speed, it MUST be removed _before_ `git worktree remove` (Windows junction + worktree removal is a known data-loss trap).
- Never modify the main checkout or another agent's worktree; expect `main` to have advanced while you worked.
- **Completion sequence:** merge `main` into the phase branch (trivial conflicts resolved; non-trivial conflicts are a Halt & Query, §13.3.2) → re-run full verification green **inside the worktree** → from the main checkout, `git merge --no-ff phase-<N>-<slug>` → `git worktree remove` the worktree → delete the branch.

### 13.4 Protocol Delta — Blast Radius & Rollback

Before any multi-file fix, note the intended blast radius (files + tables). Maximum **two** autonomous attempts at a given bug; on the second failure, `git` revert to the last good state, write a root-cause analysis, and await human input. Panic-fix cascades across unrelated files are forbidden.

### 13.5 Verification Ergonomics

The `:memory:`-driver unit path and mocked bridges (§11.3) are for logic; they never prove the real OPFS/SAB/worklet path — the Playwright smoke (§11.4) is the proof and MUST stay green per phase. Audio correctness beyond "no errors" is proven by offline renders (§11.2), not by ear alone; human listening QA is the final polish gate, not the primary verification.

### 13.6 Protocol Epsilon — Anti-Drift Enforcement

Prose rules do not stop drift; gates do. The following are mechanical, not advisory:

- **Naming freeze.** Every identifier this spec names — store names, file paths (§2.5), constants (§2.6), worker/worklet filenames, message `kind`s (§7.1.3), DB tables/columns (§9.3), effect IDs (§5.7), parameter address forms (§7.8) — is binding. The agent MUST NOT rename, "improve", or synonymise them. If a name proves genuinely wrong, that is a Halt & Query, then a §14 entry.
- **Spec anchors in code.** Any implementation choice that is non-obvious without this document carries a `// spec §x.y` comment (house style). This is for the _reviewer's_ traceability — it is the one sanctioned "why" comment form.
- **Green-before-next.** `type-check`, `lint`, and the unit tests covering the touched area MUST pass before the next task begins; the full suite plus the browser smoke MUST pass at phase exit. Building on top of a red build is forbidden.
- **Enforcement scripts** (Phase 0 deliverables, aggregated as `npm run verify`, run at every phase exit):
  - `check:deps` — fails if `package.json` contains any package outside the §2.2 matrix, or any forbidden package.
  - `check:lang` — scans identifiers and UI strings for American spellings (color/behavior/initialize/synchronize/normalize…) with an explicit allowlist file for platform API names (`AnalyserNode` is British already; `normalize()` the string method, `KeyboardEvent.getModifierState` etc. are platform-fixed and allowlisted).
  - `check:stubs` — every temporary stub or deferred wiring is tagged `// STUB(phase-N): reason`. The script lists open stubs (they must appear in the handover) and **fails** from Phase 7 onward if any remain. It also **fails** on any phase reference in `src` outside the historical phrasings allowlisted in `scripts/check-stubs.allowlist.json` (exit-criterion citations, §14 provenance notes, frozen identifiers). A deferral written as prose — "the polished editor is Phase 7" — satisfied the tag gate while the work was outstanding; naming a completed phase describes no schedule, so a comment states what the code does now and links the tracking issue for whatever is genuinely left.
- **In-repo precedent rule.** The DB worker, RPC bridge, `sw.ts`, `vite.config.ts`, and the PWA update prompt are written and proven; they are the reference patterns for this codebase. Before adding anything adjacent to them, read the existing file and extend its pattern rather than designing a parallel one. (This rule previously pointed at an external reference repository, retired in §14 2026-07-18 (l).)
- **No invented documentation.** Uncertain API ⇒ read `node_modules` types or the existing call sites in this repo (§2.7); still uncertain ⇒ Halt & Query. Writing a call site from memory that contradicts installed types is treated as a failed review, not a style issue.
- **Commit cadence.** Small commits at each green milestone; the working tree is clean at every session end; messages describe _what/why_ only.

### 13.7 Canonical Session Prompt Template

Each phase runs in a fresh session bootstrapped by a **self-contained prompt**. The outgoing agent generates the next one from this exact template (fill `<N>`, `<N+1>`, `<PHASE_NAME>`, `<slug>`; keep everything else verbatim; drop the two "[Phase 0 only: …]" notes for phases ≥ 1). The generated prompt MUST be the final thing in the agent's last message, in a raw fenced markdown block (§13.1).

```markdown
# BangerBox — Phase <N> Implementation Session

You are implementing **BangerBox**, an offline-first PWA DAW/sequencer/sampler. Work in
the repository root you have been opened in.

## Before writing any code (mandatory, in this order)

1. Read the **entire** specification at `docs/todo/_spec.md`. It is the single, binding
   source of truth — every schema, locked decision (§1.3), pinned API (§2.7), and
   protocol (§13) applies to this session.
2. Read `docs/dev/PHASE_HANDOVER.md` fully (Protocol Alpha, §13.1) and reuse its
   established patterns — never invent parallel ones. [Phase 0 only: it will not exist
   yet — proceed on the spec alone.]
3. Set up your isolated worktree (§13.3.4): branch `phase-<N>-<slug>`, worktree at
   `.claude/worktrees/phase-<N>`, its own `npm install`. ALL work happens inside that
   worktree — never in the main checkout, which other agents may be using concurrently.
   [Phase 0 only: the repo does not exist yet — first `git init` + an initial commit on
   `main` in the project root, then create the worktree.]
4. Post a brief high-level execution plan for the phase before touching the file system.

## Task

Execute **Phase <N> — <PHASE_NAME>** (spec §12) autonomously and end-to-end under the
spec §13 protocols: continuous execution, halting only at the §13.3.2 thresholds; TDD
(§13.2); two-strike rollback (§13.4); anti-drift gates (§13.6 — naming freeze,
`// spec §x.y` anchors, green-before-next, `npm run verify`). **Never write a library
call from memory** — use the Pinned API Contract (§2.7), the installed `.d.ts` under
`node_modules/`, or an existing working call site in this repo.
British English throughout (§3.7). The repo is public: no secrets, no personal data;
small green-milestone commits describing what/why only.

## Definition of done

Every Phase <N> exit criterion in spec §12 is met, including the Multi-Lens Review
(§3.5), with all verification commands green inside the worktree.

## End-of-session obligations (in order)

1. Land the phase per §13.3.4: merge `main` into the phase branch, re-verify green,
   merge the phase branch into `main` with `--no-ff` from the main checkout, remove the
   worktree, delete the branch.
2. Regenerate `docs/dev/PHASE_HANDOVER.md` on `main` (Protocol Alpha, §13.1).
3. Report results honestly — including anything skipped, stubbed, or failing.
4. End your final message with the **Phase <N+1>** continuation prompt, generated from
   spec §13.7 with placeholders filled, in a raw fenced markdown block — then STOP.
   Do not begin Phase <N+1>. [After the final phase: state that the chain is complete
   instead of emitting a prompt.]
```

## 14. Changelog

- **2026-09-05 (av)** — _Two tracks that play one program each get their OWN §5.2 pad channel, and the §4.2 strip that supplies its values stays per program (§4.2, §5.2, §5.3, §8.5.6, §9.5). Closes issue #141. Replaces `MixerGraph.getChannel` with `channelsFor`, adds a return shape on `ensurePadChannel`, one method on `AudioBridge`, and one §11.4 probe seam with the smoke step that drives it. No §4.2 channel-id FORM, §7.8 address, §6 payload shape, schema field, DDL column, §7.1.3 message kind or store slice changed, `PRAGMA user_version` is unchanged and **no §9.2 migration was added**._ **(1) The gap.** All eight §12 phases are complete, so this is defect closure against §5.2. A pad channel was keyed `pad:<programId>:<padIndex>` — no track in the id — and `ensurePadChannel` returned the existing handle and IGNORED its `trackInput` argument once one was built. §5.2 stage 5 places "all pad outputs of the program ON A TRACK" at that track's input, so two tracks playing one program shared one node, wired to whichever triggered it first. The second track's voices arrived at the FIRST track's strip: its own fader, pan, mute, solo, four sends and insert chain were bypassed for every pad the two shared, and deleting the first track destroyed the node the second was sounding through. Measured, over real OPFS, on a bar where both tracks hit the same pad in unison: closing the second track's fader rendered **0.06162 RMS against 0.06162** — the same number to every digit, ×1.0000 — and closing the first rendered **0**. Neither fader did what a fader means. **(2) A §4.2 channel id NAMES a strip; the graph REALISES it, once per track.** That distinction is the whole of the fix, and it is what makes the blast radius small rather than a rewrite of the §7.8 grammar. ⚑ **§5.2 stage 5 forces one signal path per TRACK: a `GainNode` has one output, so no single node can send track A's voices to track A's input and track B's to track B's.** ⚑ **§6 forces one set of VALUES per PROGRAM: a pad's mixer and inserts live in the §6 `Pad` record, which belongs to the program, and the §9.3 `tracks.mixer` column holds one `ChannelStrip` for the track itself.** Both are binding and neither can bend, so the answer is neither "the strip becomes per-track" nor "one channel sums into every track" — it is that the two questions were never the same question. One §6 `Pad` record, one §4.2 strip, one §7.8 address, N realisations. `MixerGraph.channelsFor` is what keeps the difference invisible to every writer: it replaces `getChannel`, returns 0..N, and the four §4.2 strip writers, the §5.2 mute application and both §7.8 dispatch paths all iterate it. The alternative — keying the STRIP by track — would have changed the §7.8 address grammar, needed somewhere in §6 to put a second set of values, and given §8.5.6's Pads tab a track selector it has no business having. **(3) What a §7.8 lane saved against the old address means afterwards: exactly what it always meant.** `mixer.pad:<prog>:<idx>.level` still addresses that pad's fader in that program. The address's MEANING is unchanged, because the strip it names is still per program — what changed is that the ramp now reaches every track playing the program instead of only the first one to trigger it, which is what the lane always said. There is nothing on disk in a shape to correct, no §9.2 migration, and no §14 (ap) reconciliation to make: a project written before this holds the same value, addressed the same way, and simply becomes audible on the tracks it was already meant to describe. The same is true of a §10.3 binding on that address. **(4) §8.5.6 needs no track selector, and the tab is honest without one.** The Pads tab renders a strip per assigned pad of the ACTIVE PROGRAM and has no notion of a track (§8.5.6). That was accurate before the fix only by accident — one strip, one channel — and it is accurate afterwards by construction: there is still exactly one set of values per pad, and it now applies to every track that plays the program. A selector would assert per-track pad values, which §6 has nowhere to store and which no §7.8 address could name. The control that WAS inert is on the Tracks tab: the second track's own strip, which is what this fixes. **(5) The graph reports what it BUILT, and two "already seeded" sets are gone.** `ensurePadChannel` returns `{ channel, created }`. `AudioEngine.channelMixerApplied` and `bounceService.padMixerApplied` were both keyed by channel id with no track, so neither could have counted realisations — and the engine's outlived `removePadChannel`, so a rebuilt channel kept the §4.2 defaults `createChannelStrip` gives it rather than its §6 values. The graph owns a channel's lifetime, so it is the only thing that can answer the question. **(6) EVERY lazily built channel is SEEDED, and that is a hole the fix would otherwise have widened.** A track channel is built on the track's first note and a pad realisation on the first hit that needs it — both long after the single `resyncAll` that `startAudioEngine` runs, and `mixerSync` pushes only what CHANGED. `AudioBridge.seedChannel` writes the §4.2 strip and the §5.2 effective mute onto that ONE channel, in the §14 (ar) order: the §6 payload first (the only value there is for a pad of a program that is not the active one), then the store, which §1.3 #16 makes the runtime truth. ⚑ **It is per-channel rather than a narrowed `resyncAll` because `applyInserts` DESTROYS and rebuilds a chain**: resyncing by id would glitch the realisation that is already sounding. ⚑ **Inserts are the part only this can supply.** `ResolvedVoice` carries a pad's `mixer` but not its `inserts`, so before this a pad channel built after the user added an effect carried none until the next store write — pre-existing, and made reachable by default the moment a second realisation could appear. ⚑ **The chain is wired on a MICROTASK, and that is a §11.5 requirement rather than an optimisation** — see item (14). Both halves are measured rather than argued: an 80 Hz lowpass in the pad's own rack before the first note takes the live master peak from **0.27213** to **0.00276** (×0.020), two octaves below the tone. **(7) A deleted track takes its own pad realisations with it (§3.2).** They are connected to the input node `removeTrackChannel` destroys, so leaving them would be an orphan on a dead branch whose sends still feed the returns. That is also the second symptom of #141 in its fixed form: deleting one track no longer touches what the other sounds through. **(8) What stays PROGRAM-scoped, on purpose.** §5.4 scopes choke groups "within the program" and mono retrigger to the pad, and `VoicePool` keys both on `padKey` = `${programId}:${padIndex}` with no track in it. Two tracks playing one mono pad therefore still cut each other off, and a choke group still chokes across them. That is the spec's own reading rather than an oversight, and it is left alone. The §7.8 per-voice program addresses (`program:<id>.pad:<idx>.filter.cutoff`) reach voices through the same key and so act on both tracks' voices, consistently. **(9) The cost, stated.** N tracks playing one program now build N pad chains for each pad they share, each with its own insert rack. §11.5 has no size budget and none is being reintroduced; the runtime budgets do bind, and this is the price §5.2 stage 5 charges — a pad that sounds on two tracks is two signal paths, however it is stored. Nothing is built until a track actually plays the pad. **(10) A §9.5 render inherits the fix for free**, because §14 (ar) made the offline graph the live one. A stem of one of two tracks on a shared program now carries only that track's voices, where it previously carried both or none; `bounceService` changed only where it passes the track id through. **(11) What the tests now pin.** 1965 unit tests, up from 1949, across 177 files. `graph.test.ts` gains six: two tracks get distinct realisations each reaching its own input and both carrying the same §4.2 id; `channelsFor` resolves the id to both; the tempo fan-out and `allChannels` see both; `removeTrackChannel` takes one and leaves the other reaching its input; `removePadChannel` takes both and a rebuild reports `created` again; and a full teardown leaves no live node and no pending param. `audioBridge.test.ts` gains six: a level, a pan, a send and a §7.8 ramp each reaching both realisations; a solo elsewhere muting both; `seedChannel` writing a strip and the derived mute onto one fresh channel; `seedChannel` writing only the mute where the store has no strip; the insert chain arriving on a microtask rather than synchronously; and the deferred chain being dropped when the channel has gone. **Every regression test was proven against the code it was written to catch**, by six mutations: `ensurePadChannel` keying by the channel id alone — the defect as filed (4 failures); the bridge writing to `channelsFor(id)[0]` (2); `seedChannel` applying no strip (1); `removeTrackChannel` leaving the track's pad realisations (1); `seedChannel` applying its chain synchronously (2); and `trackChannel` seeding nothing, which is browser-only and is measured in item (14). **(12) Measured in a real browser** (spec §13.5), not inferred: the full §11.4 smoke at ports 5342/5343, **78/78 steps** (up from 76), dev and offline, no console errors. Both tracks hit one pad on the same four beats of a bar of 4/4 at 120 bpm, so every hit is two coherent voices; each §9.5 bounce is read back from `/bounces/` over real OPFS. Both faders at unity measured **0.06162 RMS**; closing the SECOND track's fader measured **0.03081** (×0.5000) and closing the FIRST measured **0.03081** (×0.5000) — one of two unison voices, from either side. The pad strip's own fader at 0.8 measured ×0.2512, which is what §8.5.6's law says −12 dB is and which the defect also produced, so it is a guard rather than a regression test. Live, through `AudioEngine` rather than `bounceService`, the §5.8 master peak read **0.13589** with the second track's fader at 0 against **0.27213** at unity (×0.499) — and that fader was set BEFORE its channel existed, so the same reading covers item (14)'s first finding; the pad's own 80 Hz lowpass, also placed before its realisations existed, held that peak at **0.00276** (×0.020); and the graph held **2** channels under the one pad id. **Against the unfixed build the same step reports the defect exactly**: 1 realisation, `secondFaderClosedRms` **0.061621618782488896** against a `bothTracksRms` of **0.061621618782488896** — identical to every digit — `firstFaderClosedRms` **0**, and a live peak of 0.27392 → 0.26976 (×0.985). The step fails on the second track's fader, which is the audio claim and is reached before the realisation count. **(13) Scope and honest limits.** ⚑ **A pad's `mute` and `solo` are still session state**, because §6's `Pad.mixer` defines no field for them (§14 (as) item 6); a realisation is muted by the derived §5.2 result like any other channel, and that result is not persisted. ⚑ **A keygroup program's program-scope strip is still unreachable** (issue #139), and unaffected: `padStripsForProgram` publishes no strip for it either way. ⚑ **The `pad:<trackId>:<note>` channel the demo fallback builds is unchanged** — it already carried a track in its id, so it has exactly one realisation, and it is not a §6 pad. ⚑ **`removeInsert` still shrinks the rack** (issue #142), which is why the §11.4 step BYPASSES its probe filter rather than removing it. ⚑ **`seedChannel` has no call from a §9.5 render**, because a render creates every channel it needs before its one `resyncAll` pass and §14 (ar) records that order. ⚑ **The very first hit of a pad whose rack was edited before its realisation existed can pass a few milliseconds of DRY signal**, because the microtask that wires the chain is itself what costs — a reverb's impulse response can outlast a render quantum. That is the trade item (14) makes deliberately, and the alternative is a stall on the audition path.

**(14) What the review caught, and what each answer is.** Two findings, both fixed, and both are about a channel this work did not create. ⚑ **A TRACK channel is built lazily too, and nothing had ever seeded it.** `startAudioEngine` runs its one `resyncAll` before any track channel exists — they are built on a track's first note — and `mixerSync` pushes only what CHANGED, so a project loaded with a track fader at 0.3, a pan, an open send, an insert rack or `mute: true` played that track at unity, centred, dry and unmuted until the user touched the control. Pre-existing and no part of #141, but this work put the mechanism that closes it one line away and applied it to the pad alone. `ensureTrackChannel` now returns the same `EnsuredChannel` and `AudioEngine.trackChannel` seeds a new one. **Measured** rather than inspected: with the second track's fader committed to 0 before its channel existed, the live master peak read **0.13589** against **0.27213** at unity (×0.499); against the unseeded build the same reading was **0.27618** against **0.27960** (×0.988) — the fader reached nothing. ⚑ **The insert chain is wired on a MICROTASK, because `seedChannel` runs on §7.6's audition path.** `triggerLiveNote` → `soundResolvedVoice` → `ensureProgramChannel` → the pool, with `when` = now; and `createInsert('reverb')` synthesises its impulse response on the main thread through `makeReverbImpulse` — up to ten seconds of stereo audio. Building it before `voicePool.trigger` would have spent §11.5's whole 30 ms touch-to-sound budget on the first hit of a pad that carries one, where the §6 payload seed it replaced was three parameter writes. The voice's start time is fixed before the chain is built, so the note sounds on time and the chain is wired within the same turn — and the microtask asks the graph whether it still holds the channel, because a program change or a track delete may have destroyed it in between (§3.2, §5.3).

- **2026-09-05 (au)** — _A channel's insert chain is bounded by the §1.3.1 slot limit, and that limit is CONFIGURABLE for the first time (§1.3.1, §4.2, §5.7, §8.5.1, §8.5.6, §9.3). Closes issue #135. Adds one store result type, one exported pure rule, one §8.5.1 control and one §11.4 probe seam with the smoke step that drives it. No schema field, DDL column, §7.1.3 message kind, store slice or §6 payload SHAPE changed, `PRAGMA user_version` is unchanged and **no §9.2 migration was added**._ **(1) The gap is wider than the issue's title.** All eight §12 phases are complete, so this is defect closure in `src/store/` again. `addInsert` wrote `[...prev.inserts, slot]` and never read `globalInsertLimit` — but `createDefaultChannelStrip` opens the §1.3.1 rack with FOUR EMPTY SLOTS, so appending put the very FIRST effect a user added on slot 5, with four empty rows visible above it, and the fifth on slot 9. Past slot 8 `parseParamTarget` returns null, because `GLOBAL_INSERT_LIMIT_RANGE` bounds the §7.8 grammar's `slotN` — so `useMixerStore.commit` returns before writing, `channelAutomatableParams` offers nothing, and the §8.5.6 panel's own knobs, every §8.5.2 lane, every §8.5.10 axis and every §10.3 binding on that slot address nothing while the effect goes on sounding. **Measured in a real browser rather than inferred, on both sides.** Twelve adds on the master strip left **16 slots, 12 of them occupied, and no refusal at all**; slots **9 to 16** held an effect no §7.8 address could reach; and closing slot 16 through `insert:master:slot16.cutoff` left the §5.8 master peak at **1.47944235801696777**, the same number to every digit it had read before the write. With the limit enforced the same twelve adds leave 4 slots, and closing slot 4 through its own address takes the peak from **0.49939 to 0.00320**. **(2) The limit bounds a CHANNEL, and one rule covers every strip.** §1.3.1's own wording is "4 insert slots per pad, per track, and on the master", so it is per channel and not a project total. ⚑ **The §5.2 RETURNS are bounded too, and that is one rule rather than two.** §1.3.1 does not name them and §5.2 gives them no insert stage of their own, but they have §4.2 strips, §8.5.6 edits their inserts on its own tab, and `insert:return:0:slot9.mix` goes dead exactly as a track's does. A second rule would be a second thing to forget. **(3) The limit is applied where a slot is CREATED, which is §14 (ap)'s own two places.** ⚑ **`addInsert` FILLS the first free slot of the rack** and appends only while the rack is shorter than the limit. Refusing without filling would have made the picker dead on every default channel — four empty slots and nothing may be added to them — which is the §3.4 failure the fix exists to prevent. The filled slot keeps its own id, exactly as `replaceInsert` does: the id is the slot's handle. ⚑ **`replaceInsert` refuses to OCCUPY a position at or past the limit.** Only a chain admitted from a project has a slot there at all, and filling one would put a sounding effect where no §7.8 address reaches. ⚑ **A REORDER needs no rule.** `InsertPanel.moveSlot` neither creates a slot nor lengthens the array, so within the limit it cannot push anything past one. **(4) The refusal is SPOKEN, not silent.** `addInsert` returned `void`, so a full channel swallowed the request and the picker read as broken. Both actions now return `InsertSlotResult` — `{ ok: true } | { ok: false, reason }`, the `AssignResult` and `AutomationEditResult` shape — carrying a finished sentence, because the store is the only layer that knows which rule refused. §8.5.6 shows it as a `warning` toast and additionally DISABLES the Add picker on a full channel and the Replace select on a slot past the limit, from the same exported `freeInsertSlot` the store refuses by rather than from a restatement of it. That is the `LayersEditor` shape for a §6 cap. The toast path stays reachable for the refusal the disabling cannot cover — a channel with no strip at all, where the panel has no chain to judge. **(5) A chain ALREADY over the limit is admitted WHOLE, and nothing is repaired.** ⚑ Three answers were available and this is §14 (ap)'s "the stored value always wins" one level up. **Refusing** would make a project unopenable over an insert slot; **truncating** would silently delete an effect the user made, and a §9.5 bounce would then render differently from the one they last heard. So a §9.6 import, a §9.8 pack or a project saved before this loads with every slot intact and every effect sounding — measured: a nine-slot chain is admitted as nine. What sits past the limit stays unaddressable, which is the pre-existing §7.8 consequence and is not made worse; nothing the app itself builds can reach that state any more. ⚑ **Lowering the limit repairs nothing either**, for the same reason: it bounds where the next effect may be created and leaves the ones already there alone. **(6) The §7.8 grammar keeps its own bound of 1–8, deliberately.** `parseParamTarget` bounds `slotN` by `GLOBAL_INSERT_LIMIT_RANGE`, the CONFIGURABLE RANGE, not by the open project's current value. The registry is pure and dependency-free (§2.5) so it may not read a store; and a project whose limit is later raised to 8 must be able to read back a slot-8 address it saved. The store is where the current value binds. **(7) Nothing could CHANGE `globalInsertLimit`, and after this it BINDS.** `setGlobalInsertLimit` had no call site outside a unit test, `mappers.ts` hydrated the §9.3 `projects.insert_limit` column and `persist.ts` wrote it back, and nothing in between read either — so the setting was inert on both sides and the omission cost nothing. Enforcing it makes 4 a real ceiling, and shipping a binding limit with no way to move it implements half of §1.3.1 — so §8.5.1's Project panel gains an **Insert slots** select beside Sample rate and Bit depth, the two other §9.3 project columns it already owns. It is in scope because the fix is what made it matter, not because the issue asked. **(8) What the tests now pin.** 1949 unit tests, up from 1925, across 177 files. `insertLimit.test.ts` drives both sides of the defect: that the chain never grows past the limit however hard it is pushed, that the refusal is a sentence, that an add fills the rack's first free slot and keeps its id, that the limit comes from the open project, that the master and the returns are bounded by the same rule, that `replaceInsert` refuses a forbidden position — and, as the §3.4 invariant it really is, that **every slot the store holds an effect in is addressable**, both through `isAutomatable` and through the `channelAutomatableParams` catalogue a picker reads. **Every regression test was proven against the code it was written to catch**: 15 of the 19 assertions fail against the unfixed store, and the 4 that pass are guards — the two admission cases that must keep working, the grammar's own refusal of `slot9`, and the id a fill preserves. **Eight EXISTING tests pinned the defect and were corrected rather than added to**, all of them `inserts.at(-1)` standing for "the slot an add created", which is now the first occupied slot rather than the last. **(9) Measured in a real browser** (spec §13.5), not inferred: **76/76 smoke steps** at ports 5342/5343, dev and offline, no console errors, up from 74. Twelve `addInsert` calls on the master strip left 4 slots, 4 occupied, and a refusal in words; no occupied slot was unaddressable; a looping 1 kHz tone fed straight into the §5.2 master input read **0.49939** with every slot opened to 20 kHz through its own §7.8 address and **0.00320** with slot 4 alone closed to 80 Hz through the same address; a nine-slot chain was admitted whole. **Against the unfixed store the step fails on the COUNT assertion** — "twelve adds left 16 slots (12 occupied) on master — §1.3.1 allows 4" — which is reached before the peak, so the numbers in item (1) were read by printing the probe's own result rather than from a step that got that far. The proof restores the §9.3 `projects.payload` column verbatim and reloads: `installAudioProbe` runs in production builds. **(10) One defect found BEHIND this one, filed rather than fixed.** ⚑ **`removeInsert` DROPS the slot from the array rather than emptying it**, so every §7.8 address behind the removed slot shifts onto a different effect — the editing-side half of the defect §14 (ar) fixed for the wiring side. Pre-existing; made visible by bounding the rack, because the §11.4 smoke's insert-panel step had to stop counting slots after a removal and count effects instead. Filed as issue #142. **(11) What the review caught, and what each answer is.** Four findings, all fixed, and two of them are defects this work made REACHABLE rather than introduced. ⚑ **A §11.4 probe restores every §9.3 COLUMN it writes, not only the one it meant to.** The proof rewrote `projects.payload` and put it back, but `flushProject` writes `insert_limit` from the store as well — so a user who had chosen 8 slots lost them to the 4 the proof sets, on every run, in a production build. This is the §14 (ap) probe rule read one level up again, and the second time in three entries that the restore was narrower than the save. ⚑ **An out-of-range `insert_limit` from a project is CLAMPED where the settings enter the store.** §9.3 declares no CHECK on the column, `rowToProjectSettings` copies it through and §9.6's manifest types it as a bare `z.number()`, so a hand-edited row or an imported project could carry any value at all. That cost nothing while the value was inert; enforcing it is what made `20` mean chains no §7.8 address can reach and `0` mean a channel locked out of inserts entirely. `applyProject` clamps, which covers §4.4 hydration, a §9.6 import and a §9.8 pack at once — the same shape as `withCompleteInserts`, and the reason it is not a second clamp beside `setGlobalInsertLimit`'s. ⚑ **A refusal names the rule that actually refused.** `replaceInsert` reused `addInsert`'s “all N slots are in use” for a forbidden POSITION, which is false of a mostly empty over-long rack; it now names the slot. The §8.5.6 tooltip likewise claimed nothing could address such a slot, which is true only past the §7.8 grammar's own bound of 8 — a slot past THIS project's limit may still be addressable, and one already sounding goes on sounding. ⚑ **The REORDER is the one surface that moves an effect between §7.8 addresses without creating a slot**, so `upsertChannel` does not bound it and inside an over-long chain Move-later could walk an effect into a genuinely dead position. That button is no longer offered, and item (5)'s claim is corrected to say what is true: nothing the app builds can GROW a chain into that state, and §8.5.6 declines to walk one into it.

- **2026-09-05 (at)** — _A deleted track is WITHDRAWN from the §7.1.3 worker, and takes with it the two things the §9.3 `tracks` row does not cascade (§7.1.3, §7.5, §7.8, §9.3). Closes issue #137. Adds one §7.1.3 request KIND (`removeTrack`), one `SchedulerCore` method, one `sequencerSync` subscriber and one §11.4 probe seam with the smoke step that drives it. No schema field, DDL column, existing message kind's SHAPE, store slice or §6 payload shape changed, `SCHEDULER_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is unchanged at 2, and **no §9.2 migration was added**._ **(1) The gap.** All eight §12 phases are complete, so this is defect closure in `src/store/syncLayer/` and `src/core/sequencer/` again. `subscribeSequencerSync`'s events subscriber iterated `Object.entries(events)` and never handled a REMOVED key, where the automation subscriber immediately below it does — so `removeTrack` deleted the store's track and its events and told the worker nothing, and the worker kept scheduling those events until the project was reloaded. Measured in a real browser rather than inferred, on both sides of the fix: with the sender's withdrawal removed, deleting a track mid-transport still had the worker scheduling BOTH track ids a full lookahead later; with it, the §5.8 master peak fell from 0.19070 to 0.05512 and the batch carried one id. **(2) The withdrawal is a NEW kind, not an `eventsDiff` listing every id in `deletes`.** ⚑ **`eventsDiff` is a diff of a track's EVENTS, and a deleted track has neither events nor the `sequenceId` that kind carries.** `diffTrackEvents` would have had to invent one — it reads `tracks[trackId]?.sequenceId ?? ''` — and `applyEventsDiff` builds its entry from `?? { sequenceId, events: [] }`, so a pure-delete diff for a track already absent RE-CREATES the map entry it was sent to empty. ⚑ **It could not reach the rest of what the worker keys by a track id.** The §7.5 groove, the §7.6 held notes, the §7.7 open capture and the armed erase are none of them events, and a capture flush reaches the store through `commitRecordedTake`, which writes the deleted track's events map key straight back. ⚑ **Adding a kind does not move the version**, which is the extend-by-adding-kinds rule §14 (ao) states in as many words; `SCHEDULER_PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays at 2, and an older peer that does not know `removeTrack` behaves exactly as this build did before the fix. **(3) The rule is the SENDER's, and this does not re-open §14 (aq).** ⚑ **(aq) settled SELECTION** — which of the tracks the worker holds should sound now — and the answer was that the sender ships the whole project, because song mode selects a different sequence per SEGMENT and narrowing at the sender would empty song mode and turn every switch of active sequence into the full re-send §7.1.3 forbids. ⚑ **A withdrawal leaves nothing to select.** No mode plays the track again, in any segment, ever; there is no later moment at which the worker would want it. ⚑ **And the worker cannot work it out for itself.** Nothing in its state distinguishes "a track of a sequence that is not playing" from "a track that no longer exists" — which is precisely why (aq)'s worker-side filter was right and this one would not be. `sequencerSync.test.ts`'s test named for (aq) is untouched and still passes: the sender still forwards an `eventsDiff` for every track in the project. **(4) The subscriber watches `tracks`, not `events`.** A track deleted before it was ever played never entered the events map, so a removed-key loop there would find nothing to withdraw — while the worker may still hold that track's groove. `tracks` is where "the project no longer has this" is observable. It also fixes a leak nobody had filed: `subscribeSequencerSync` is registered once at the §5.1 start gate and survives a project switch, so every track of every project opened in a session accumulated in the worker's map. **(5) A track deleted while the transport is ROLLING.** Notes already posted into the §7.1.4 lookahead window are still DISPATCHED — the batch carrying them has left the worker, which is the same window a §7.7 live erase cannot reach back into, and re-timing or recalling a posted batch is not something the scheduler does. They are SILENT, though, and that is the review's correction to a claim this entry first made the other way: the store no longer holds the track, so `resolveNote` finds no program, and `triggerFallbackDemo` now refuses a track the project does not have. ⚑ **A track with no PROGRAM and a track that does not EXIST are different things**, and the §12 demo fallback only ever meant the first. Sounding the second played the bundled demo sample instead of the track, and — worse — `ensureTrackChannel` rebuilt the §5.2 channel `deleteTrack` had just destroyed, leaving it and a fresh pad channel wired to the master bus for the session, which §3.2 calls a critical failure. Pre-existing, and reachable before this work for every note of a deleted track rather than for one lookahead window; the browser proof now reads `graph.getChannel` after the settle. The proof still waits 400 ms before measuring, because the scheduled-batch reading needs the window to have passed. **(6) A track OWNS two things the §9.3 `tracks` row does not cascade, and `useSequenceStore.removeTrack` now takes both.** ⚑ **Its §7.8 automation lanes — the ones it OWNS and the ones that ADDRESS it.** Owning is track scope with this id as `ownerId`. Addressing is any lane, of either scope, whose target names the track's §4.2 channel; the registry answers which channel a target names, so the §7.8 grammar keeps one owner (§13.6), and `bouncePlan` already asks the same question the same way. Both halves are needed, which is the review's second correction: `recordParamGesture` writes SEQUENCE-scope lanes, so a fader ridden on a track and captured is owned by the sequence and would have survived its subject for ever, addressing a `crypto.randomUUID()` channel id that can never exist again. `automation_points` declares no foreign key at all, so nothing else would ever have removed either kind — and they are worse than orphan data: `laneForTarget` lets a track lane override a sequence lane on the same §7.8 address whatever track owns it. Each emptied lane gets its own §4.4 dirty key, carrying that lane's OWN scope and owner rather than the track's, or the flush replaces the wrong rows and the orphans stay. ⚑ **Its §7.5 groove assignment**, which lives in the §9.3 `projects.payload` rather than on the track row, and so reached a §9.6 export as an orphan key. ⚑ **Both go in the STORE ACTION, not in `projectCrud.deleteTrack`**, so `deleteSequence`'s loop and any later caller get the same rule. ⚑ **Both reach the worker on the paths they already have** — the `automation` subscriber's cleared-key loop and the `trackGrooveIds` one — so the new message says only what those cannot. ⚑ **The revert restores exactly what the delete took**, captured at the delete rather than as whole maps, so an unrelated lane edited between the delete and the undo stands. **(7) What was already correct, checked rather than assumed.** The §4.2 `track:<id>` strip goes: `deleteTrack` calls `commitTrackStrip(id, false)`, `mixerSync`'s own removed-key loop calls `bridge.removeChannel`, and that reaches `graph.removeTrackChannel`. The `midi_events` rows cascade from the `tracks` row. A §9.6 export never carried the orphan lanes, because `dumpSnapshot` walks the SURVIVING tracks — it carried the orphan groove key, which is now gone too. **(8) What the tests now pin.** 1925 unit tests, up from 1907, across 176 files. `trackWithdrawal.test.ts` drives the real core through `parseSchedulerRequest` + `applySchedulerRequest`, because a sender whose kind has no schema member is dropped in silence at the worker boundary (issue #71). **Every regression test was proven against the code it was written to catch**, by five mutations: no `tracks` subscriber at all — the defect as filed (3 failures); the withdrawal sent from the EVENTS subscriber instead (2); a core that forgets the track and nothing else (3); a core that ignores the withdrawal entirely (6); and a store that keeps the deleted track's lanes and groove (4). Two of the eight core tests are GUARDS rather than regression tests and no mutation moves them: a withdrawal for a track the core never held, and an events diff arriving after a withdrawal. **The defect itself was measured in the browser too**, by removing the sender's withdrawal and re-running: the smoke step fails on the WIRE assertion — the worker scheduled both track ids a full lookahead after the delete — which is reached before the peak, so the peak under the defect was not read. **(9) Measured in a real browser** (spec §13.5), not inferred: 74/74 smoke steps at ports 5342/5343, both the dev and the offline pass, no console errors. Two tracks, a program each, the deleted one's pad five times the survivor's; deleting it mid-transport took the master peak from 0.19070 to 0.05512 (×0.289) in the dev pass and 0.16801 to 0.04800 (×0.286) in the offline one, and left the survivor audible; a `saveNow()` afterwards left no `tracks` row, no `midi_events` row, no `automation_points` row, no `trackGrooveIds` key, no §4.2 strip and no §5.2 channel in the graph. **(10) One defect found BEHIND this one, filed rather than fixed.** ⚑ **Two tracks that play the same program share ONE §5.2 pad channel**, wired to whichever track triggered it first, so the second track's fader, pan, mute, solo, sends and inserts do nothing for that pad — and deleting the first track silences the second. The proof's first draft gave both tracks one program and measured ×0.665 where the surviving track alone should have been about half; giving each its own program was what made it measure the withdrawal instead. It is an audio-graph ownership question — what `pad:<prog>:<idx>` addresses, and so what a §7.8 lane on it means — rather than a scheduler one, and it is out of this issue's scope. Filed as issue #141. **(11) What the review caught, and what each answer is.** Three findings, all fixed, and the first two are the two halves of "what does a deleted track take with it" that the first pass answered too narrowly. ⚑ **A lane that ADDRESSES the track is the track's too**, not only one it owns — item (6). Proven by mutation: reclaiming only owned lanes fails the new assertion, and a dirty key naming the track's scope rather than the lane's fails it too, once the spy is cleared before the removal (it was not, and passed on the key the lane's own creation had already written). ⚑ **A residual note must sound NOTHING, not the demo sample** — item (5). This is the finding the fix made VISIBLE rather than introduced: it was reachable before for every note of a deleted track, and the fix shrank it to one lookahead window, which is what made the false claim in item (5) worth correcting rather than merely imprecise. ⚑ **A §11.4 probe restores the `projects.payload` COLUMN, not just the store values it wrote.** The proof adds a §7.5 groove template and `flushProject` writes the whole column from the stores, so clearing the assignment alone left the template in the real project's §8.5.2 picker. The column is put back verbatim after the last save, which is the (ap) rule read one level up.

- **2026-09-04 (as)** — _A pad's §4.2 mixer strip is reachable and it persists, through one store→store mirror owning both directions of the §6 ↔ §4.2 mapping (§4.2, §4.3, §6, §9.3, §9.5). Closes issue #133. Moves `padStrips.ts` out of `syncLayer/`, adds one `derive/` module, two exports beside `padStripsForProgram` and one `useProgramStore` action, and one §11.4 probe seam with the smoke step that drives it. No schema field, DDL column, §7.1.3 message kind or field, store slice or §6 payload SHAPE changed, `PRAGMA user_version` is unchanged and **no §9.2 migration was added**._ **(1) The gap is two gaps, and the issue names only the second.** All eight §12 phases are complete, so this is defect closure against §4.2 and §6. ⚑ **A project just loaded had NO pad strip at all.** `programSync` published them on a CHANGE of `activeProgramId`, and §4.4 hydration sets that value before the §4.3 subscribers register — so on every fresh load `useMixerStore.channels` held `master`, four returns and the track strips and nothing else. §8.5.6's Pads tab renders a strip per assigned pad regardless, falling back to `state?.level ?? 1`, so it LOOKED right; but `useMixerStore.resolvePath` found no strip, `readScalar` returned null, and `setTransient` and `commit` both returned before writing anything. Every fader, pan knob, send dial, mute, solo and slot picker on that tab did nothing whatsoever until the user happened to select another program and come back. ⚑ **And where a strip did exist, nothing wrote it back.** `mixerChannelDirtyKey` marks `program:<id>` dirty for a `pad:` channel — correctly — but `flushProgram` serialises `useProgramStore`, which the mixer never wrote to, so the flush stored the pad unchanged and cleared the unsaved dot. Measured, over real OPFS: a −12 dB pad fader saved and reloaded rendered a bounce byte-for-byte as loud as the unedited one. **(2) The write-back is store → store, which is why it is NOT the §4.3 sync layer.** §4.3 exists so that ONE place touches audio nodes in response to state; a store→store subscriber filed under it makes that sentence mean less every time. `src/store/derive/transportMirror.ts` is the standing precedent and says so in as many words, and `src/store/derive/padStripMirror.ts` sits beside it, registered by `startProjectSession` and disposed by `stopProjectSession`. ⚑ **The publish half MOVED there too, and that is the point rather than tidying.** It was already a store→store write (`programSync` called `useMixerStore.upsertChannel`) living in the sync layer for want of anywhere else. One module now owns both directions of one mapping, so neither can be forgotten when §6 or §4.2 gains a field. `subscribeProgramSync` was left with nothing but a call to `bridge.onActiveProgramChanged`, and both are deleted — see item (12). **(3) Not in `useMixerStore.commit`, and not in `flushProgram`.** ⚑ **A strip reaches the store through SEVEN actions**, not one: `commit`, `setMute`, `setSolo`, `addInsert`, `replaceInsert`, `removeInsert`, `setInsertEnabled`, plus `upsertChannel`, which §8.5.6's insert REORDER calls directly. Writing through from `commit` would have covered the fader and left the insert chain — the half issue #133 is titled after — behind, and the eighth route added later would be the one that is forgotten. A subscriber on `channels` sees all of them by construction, which is the same argument `mixerSync` already makes one layer down. ⚑ **`flushProgram` is the wrong place for the opposite reason**: it would make the payload right on disk and leave the two stores disagreeing everywhere else — in a §9.5 render, in `ensureProgramChannel`'s seeding, in a §9.6 export's `dumpSnapshot` — and §1.3 #16 makes Zustand the runtime truth precisely so that nothing has to reconcile it at the storage boundary. **(4) Only what each side CHANGED is written, and that is load-bearing rather than an optimisation.** `program:<id>.pad:<idx>.amp` and `mixer.pad:<id>:<idx>.level` are two registered §7.8 addresses for one value: `voiceParams.programParamChange` maps `amp`/`pan` to `channelLevel`/`channelPan`, so both reach the same `AudioParam` and the same §6 field. Only the mixer side writes through. A mirror that copied whole strips would therefore carry a strip's stale level back over a program-side edit on the next unrelated touch of that strip's pan — turning a display disagreement into data loss. `padStripEdit` reports the moved fields and nothing else, which is the rule `transportMirror` already states for the §4.2 tempo mirror. The remaining asymmetry — the fader reads stale until a reload — is issue #140. **(5) The publish is driven from the MIXER side as well as the program side, and both are needed.** §4.4 hydration calls `setPrograms` before `setChannels`, so a program-side publish alone writes the new project's strips into the outgoing channel map and `setChannels` then wipes them; and `loadProject` on the project ALREADY OPEN re-selects the same `activeProgramId`, which a `subscribeWithSelector` selector does not report as a change at all. Watching the map that has to hold the strips answers both, and the "never clobber an existing strip" guard is what bounds the re-entrancy: an `upsertChannel` re-enters the same subscription, finds every strip present and writes nothing. **(6) Two things are deliberately out, and neither is an oversight.** ⚑ **`mute` and `solo` are not mirrored, because §6's `Pad.mixer` defines no field for them** — level, pan and sendLevels are the whole of it. A track's mute persists in the §9.3 `tracks.mixer` column because a track strip IS the persisted object; a pad's is a projection of a §6 record that has nowhere to put one. Adding one is a §6 schema change and a §13.6 halt, not a mirror change. ⚑ **A keygroup program's program-scope `mixer` and `inserts` are a different defect.** §6 gives it one of each rather than per-pad ones, `padStripsForProgram` publishes no strip for it and §8.5.6 renders none — so there is no edit to lose. Its values sound (`ensureProgramChannel` seeds the channel from `program.mixer`) and nothing can change them: unreachable rather than unpersisted, filed as issue #139. **(7) No §9.2 migration, and that is a property of WHERE the values already live.** A project written before this fix holds its pad mixer values in the §6 payload exactly as it always did — the strip existed only in memory, so nothing on disk is in a shape to correct, and what was lost was never written. The payload is the SOURCE the strip is derived from, so every route in already reaches it: §4.4 hydration, a §9.6 import and a §9.8 pack all read the same column. This is the §14 (ap) reasoning in its own form — put the rule where the value ENTERS, and a project already on disk resolves correctly with nothing to rewrite. **(8) §14 (ar)'s "moot until #133 makes a pad strip persist at all" is no longer moot.** A §9.5 render seeds a pad channel from the §6 payload and then overwrites it from the §4.2 store where a strip exists. Both halves now carry the same numbers, so the order stops being able to matter — and a bounce taken after a reload renders the strip the user set rather than the payload's defaults. Nothing in `bounceService` changed; what changed is that the two sources it reads agree. **(9) What the tests now pin.** 1907 unit tests, up from 1874, across 175 files (1904 before the review's three). `padStrips.test.ts` gains the reverse mapping — which fields an edit reports, that mute and solo report none, that a strip with no predecessor reports nothing, and that an unnamed field keeps the pad's own value. `padStripMirror.test.ts` drives the subscriber: the publish on registration, after a hydrate, for a pad assigned while its program is active, and never over an existing strip; the write-back of a level, a pan, a send and an insert; the single undo entry; and the four cases that must write nothing. `padStripPersist.test.ts` is the end-to-end shape over a fixture DB — all four fields through the real §4.4 queue into `programs.payload` and back onto the strip. **Every regression test was proven against the code it was written to catch**, by seven mutations: no write-back at all, the defect as filed (8 failures); the pre-fix publish, on an active-program change alone (16 failures, and the 5 that pass are the guards); a `padStripEdit` that copies the whole strip (7); a `padWithStripEdit` that never hands the same pad back (1); and a write-back through the undoable `updateProgram` (1, after the undo assertion was strengthened to read both stores — it passed before, which is what said the assertion was too weak); a solo judged across one group (2 unit failures, and the render in item (12)); and a missing dirty mark (1). **(10) Measured in a real browser** (spec §13.5), not inferred: the full §11.4 smoke at ports 5342/5343, **72/72 steps** (up from 70), no console errors. One bar of 4/4 at 120 bpm carries four hits of a 1 kHz tone on pad 0, and each bounce is read back from `/bounces/` over real OPFS. Unedited it measured **0.10244 RMS**; with the pad fader committed to 0.8, SAVED and RELOADED, the same bounce measured **0.02573** — a ratio of **0.2512**, which is what §8.5.6's law says −12 dB is, and a number only a strip that survived the reload can produce. The §6 payload on disk and the strip after the reload both read 0.8 / −0.5 / 0.6 / delay 350 ms. **Against the unfixed build the step fails on each half in turn**: with the publish as it was, `no strip for pad:… on a freshly loaded project — every control on the Pads tab is inert`; and with the publish restored but no write-back — issue #133 exactly as filed — `the strip read 1 after a save and reload — expected 0.8`. `padStripProof` owns its whole arrangement as real §9.3 ROWS — its own program, sequence and track — because a save and a reload are the two things it is about, so nothing it measures may be hydrated into the stores and left there; and it cannot borrow the project's own track, because by the time the §11.4 run reaches this step earlier steps have opened imported and factory projects and what a track points at is no longer predictable. That was found the hard way: the first run passed the dev pass and failed the offline one with "no track plays the drum program". The step also SITS beside the two §5.7 insert-defaults steps rather than after the §7.9 one, because a probe that ends on a fresh `loadProject` must not run between a probe that leaves its arrangement in the stores and a step that reads it — the Grid step reads `activeSequenceId`'s tracks, and reloading the §9.3 rows between the two left it with no canvas. **(11) Scope and honest limits.** ⚑ **A §7.8 `program:….amp` or `.pan` edit still leaves the Mixer's fader stale** until a reload — issue #140, and the reason the write-back is field-diffed rather than whole-strip. ⚑ **`mute` and `solo` on a pad strip are still session state**, per item (6). ⚑ **A keygroup's program-scope strip is still unreachable** — issue #139. ⚑ **The mirror writes on the COMMIT, never on a §4.1 transient**, because `setTransient` does not write the store at all (issue #27); a gesture in flight reaches the graph and reaches the payload when it is let go. ⚑ **Undo does not re-mark dirty**, which is true of every commit in the project and is unchanged here: the mirror follows the strip back on an undo, and whether that reaches disk depends on the same rule it always did. ⚑ **The probe leaves its imported tone sample in the library**, as `bounceMixProof` does; it deletes the three rows it created and reloads. **(12) What the review caught, and what each answer is.** Three findings, all fixed, and the first is a defect this work is what made reachable by default. ⚑ **§5.2 solo-in-place is per GROUP — pads and tracks are separate — and that is the only reading under which soloing a track is audible at all.** `computeEffectiveMutes` muted every non-soloed pad AND track whenever either was soloed, and `solo.test.ts` asserted exactly that. But a pad channel's output feeds its TRACK's input (§5.2 stage 5), so soloing a track muted every pad of the program the track plays and silenced the very track that was soloed. It was pre-existing and it was hard to reach: before this work a project just loaded held no `pad:` strip at all, so `computeEffectiveMutes` never saw one until the user switched program — and making the strips permanent would have made it the state of every session. §8.5.3's two lists and §8.5.6's two tabs are the same reading: a solo is pressed within one of them. **Measured** rather than inspected (§11.2, §13.5): against the one-group build, soloing the track rendered **0.00973 RMS** where the unsoloed render is 0.10244 — the residue of the amp release before the mute ramp settles, which is what "silent" sounds like in a §9.5 file. With the fix the soloed render is **0.10244 RMS**, identical to the unsoloed one to five places, because the track's own pads are not its rivals. ⚑ **`applyPadStripEdit` marks the program dirty itself, as every other action in this store that changes a program does.** Leaning on the `useMixerStore` commit's own `mixerChannelDirtyKey` mark looked equivalent and was not, twice over: `commit` runs `apply()` — and so the mirror's write — BEFORE it marks anything, and §8.5.6's insert REORDER reaches the store through `upsertChannel`, a bare `set` that marks nothing at all. Without the mark, a reorder reached the payload, both stores agreed in memory, the unsaved dot never lit and the old order came back on the next load. This is not a second dirty rule (§14 (ap)): the key is the same one and the §4.4 queue coalesces by key. **The same reorder on a TRACK strip is still unsaved**, which is the pre-existing half and is out of scope here. ⚑ **`subscribeProgramSync` and `SyncBridge.onActiveProgramChanged` are DELETED rather than left calling nothing.** With the publish moved to `derive/`, the subscriber's whole body was a call to a hook that is `() => {}` in both the audio bridge and the no-op one — a speculative export in §3.4's sense. The transport and Q-Link hooks that are also inert stay, because their subscribers do real work beside them (`setBpm`; loading the mode's §10.3 bindings); `bridge.ts` now says which case is which.

- **2026-09-04 (ar)** — _Every §9.5 bounce renders the §5.2 mixer, through the live graph builders on the offline context (§5.2, §9.5, §7.8). Closes issue #134. Adds one pure module (`bouncePlan.ts`), two exports beside `automationRampForWindow`, one optional predicate on `AudioBridge.resyncAll`, and one §11.4 probe seam with the smoke step that drives it. `rampParamLinear` now anchors on the param's own timeline rather than on a clock read.
